Since 2.7.0 the review shows the **whole** Dockerfile (it used to stop at line 200) and the build is sent a **staged copy of exactly the hashed files** — `_sandy_context_hash --list` is the one enumeration — so `.sandy/config`/`.secrets`, excluded from the hash, can no longer be `COPY`'d into a layer; a symlinked Dockerfile or a context edited after review is refused (§162(16-31)). Do not describe `.sandy/` as unconditionally read-only; that confident wrong claim is how #295 was found. **Item 3, decided (option C, 2.7.0): existence-gating is kept, and a session's creation is flagged at the next approval.** Always-mounting `.sandy/` `:ro` was declined — it would bring back the workspace stubs the existence gate removed. Instead the session-end sweep, seeing `.sandy/` absent from `.protected-existed-at-launch` and present now, writes `$SANDY_HOME/approvals/dockerfile-<wd16>.session-created` (context hash + date) — outside the workspace and outside every directory mounted into the container, so neither a repository nor the session can forge or delete it — and the yellow warning says the next build will be flagged. The next prompt leads with **CREATED BY A SANDY SESSION (recorded <date>)**, ahead of both other provenance lines, and says whether the content is byte-for-byte what the session left or changed since. Cleared only when the operator approves (`y`, with a `# provenance:` line kept in the approval file) or the content matches an existing approval — never silently; `N` or a headless prompt keeps it. A session killed before its cleanup ran leaves its snapshot behind, and the gate reads it before the launch's stale-snapshot sweep deletes it (`by: next-launch`). The daemon supervisor's cleanup is the same `cleanup()`, and the `--start` pre-pass calls the same gate. Guarded by §182.

**Detection is weaker than prevention.** The window is "between session end and the user's next `git pull`/`checkout`/`push`/IDE-open"; a user who ignores the exit messages can still have a payload fire on the host. Conscious trade-off — prevention required the workspace pollution, and post-session warning suits the realistic threat model where the agent is wrong, not adversarial. Pre-existing empty stub dirs from older versions are still auto-cleaned at launch under a 4-condition gate (git repo, exact name match, empty, not git-tracked).

Intentionally **not** protected: package manifests (`Makefile`, `package.json`, `pyproject.toml`, `Cargo.toml`) — the agent legitimately edits these, and they are invoked by name rather than sourced on `cd` or filesystem scan.

**`SANDY_ALLOW_WORKFLOW_EDIT=1`** removes `.github/workflows/` from the list for a project (useful for legitimate CI work). Default off — a workflow escape fires on `git push`, long after the session ends.

**Long-term direction**: `fanotify` FAN_OPEN_PERM would give true prevention with no host pollution even for absent paths (real EPERM, works on Docker Desktop's Linux VM) but needs `CAP_SYS_ADMIN` during setup and careful supervisor logic. On the roadmap, unscoped pending evidence that detection-only is insufficient.

#### Persistent symlink approval

Dangerous symlinks (absolute, or relative escapes via `..`) are surfaced at launch. First encounter prints a y/N listing each link and target; approval persists to `$SANDBOX_DIR/.sandy-approved-symlinks.list`. Afterwards: identical-or-reduced set → silent; **a new escape → hard error at launch**, naming the links, with remediation. Sandy refuses to re-prompt — a y/N that fires every session can be trained past; a hard error forces a deliberate action.

**Approvals resolve on the CLIENT's tty, before the fork (#221).** Container bring-up happens in the supervisor, whose stdin is `/dev/null`, so an approval encountered there is unanswerable — a workspace with an escaping symlink launched fine from a shell and failed under `--start`. The `SANDY_APPROVE_ONLY` pre-pass now resolves the dangerous-symlink approval too: it runs far enough to know `$SANDBOX_DIR` (where the list lives) while still exiting before the mutex, the busy-gate, and every image build; its one added side effect is a `mkdir -p "$SANDBOX_DIR"` the launch performs moments later anyway. A refusal stops `--start` with exit `6` instead of forking a supervisor certain to hit the same refusal. This fixes pty-based clients for free (sandy-ui spawns through node-pty, so `[ -t 0 ]` holds); genuinely non-TTY consumers still need a pre-granted approval (or, for the two gates it covers, `SANDY_AUTO_APPROVE_PRIVILEGED`), and fail closed with the correct guidance plus a `.fatal` marker so the `--start` client fast-fails in ~1s rather than burning its readiness timeout.

**The pre-pass covers all three gates (2.4.0, #296).** It used to stop at two: the per-project `.sandy/Dockerfile` gate sits ~2500 lines past the pre-pass exit, so under `--start` it was reached only in the supervisor and could only fail closed — there was no way to approve a project image in daemon mode at all. `_sandy_approve_only_finish` now calls `_sandy_project_dockerfile_approved` too (hoisted above it; it needs only `WORK_DIR`, `SANDY_HOME`, `sha256`). **Declining the Dockerfile is not a refusal**: `N` means "use the base image", as in the foreground, so the pass exits `0` and `--start` proceeds — unlike a declined symlink, which exits nonzero and stops `--start` with `6`. The decline reaches the supervisor as `SANDY_DOCKERFILE_DECLINED_HASH`, **bound to the context hash**, so it skips the build with one line instead of re-printing a review nobody can answer; a context edited in between falls through to the normal fail-closed path. An env carrier is safe here because the value can only make sandy build *less*.

**`sandy --approvals [--workspace P]` reports the three gates and grants none (2.7.0, #296 item 2).** A client with no terminal otherwise gets a *silently* weaker session (keys dropped, base image, `--start` still `0`). It re-execs the pre-pass with `SANDY_APPROVE_REPORT=<file>`: each gate records `approved|pending|changed|refused|not_applicable` (key **names** only) and returns before its mkdir, prompt and writer — the symlink list refresh included — so the verdict is the launch's own (combined `config`+`.secrets`, minus env-set keys), nothing is written and no Docker is needed. Exit `0` clear, `2` unresolved, `1` no report. **No grant path, by maintainer decision.** Guarded by §183.

**`SANDY_AUTO_APPROVE_PRIVILEGED` covers two of the three gates, and the asymmetry is deliberate.** It bypasses the passive-privileged keys and the Dockerfile build; the symlink gate branches only on `[ ! -t 0 ]` and ignores it. That was never written down until #296 asked; the reason it should stay is the symlink gate's own design — a *new* escape is a hard error, never a re-prompt, so no approval of new content happens without a deliberate human act, and an env-wide bypass would silently mount every future escape read-write. Guarded by §162.

### Security knobs

Each of these is documented at length in `docs/DESIGN-NOTES.md`; the security-critical ones also have dedicated documents under `docs/security/`.

#### claude.ai account connectors (`SANDY_CLAUDE_CONNECTORS`, #129)

The Claude OAuth token sandy mounts is **account-scoped**, so every claude.ai account connector the user has ever enabled (Gmail, Drive, …) was silently reachable from **every** sandbox — ambient authority crossing the per-project boundary sandy exists to draw. Found in the field. **Connectors are suppressed by default**; `SANDY_CLAUDE_CONNECTORS=1` exposes them for one instance.

The mechanism is Claude Code's `disableClaudeAiConnectors`, seeded as a **managed** key, always overwritten **in both directions** — because its resolution is *any-source-true-wins*, so an inherited `true` from the host's settings would silently defeat the opt-in. Seeded in all three settings branches (node/jq/no-tool): a security default that varies by which tools happen to be installed is not a default. Tier is value-aware (`=1` is approval-gated from a workspace). **Two honest limits**: it gates **auto-fetched** connectors only (an explicit `--mcp-config` server still follows the normal MCP trust flow), and it is **Claude-only**. Defense in depth: `claudeAiMcpEverConnected` is stripped from the `.claude.json` seed regardless. Guarded by §110.

#### Claude Code's native `/sandbox` is forced off (#126, 2.4.0)

Claude Code ships its own Bash sandbox (`sandbox.enabled`, verified against code.claude.com/docs `settings-reference`) with its **own** egress proxy, and in an unprivileged container it needs `enableWeakerNestedSandbox` — whose own caveat is that it is for when an outer container already provides the boundary. Sandy is that boundary, and its proxy is the one policy chokepoint; a second, uncoordinated proxy inside it double-prompts and muddies which posture is in force. The host `settings.json` is the merge base, so a host `sandbox.enabled: true` used to ride into every sandbox. It is now a **managed** key like `disableClaudeAiConnectors`: forced `false` every launch in all three seeding branches, and **only `enabled`** — other `sandbox.*` keys the host set are kept. **Honest limit**: that is userSettings, the lowest scope, and Claude Code takes a Boolean from the highest scope that sets it, so a workspace's committed `.claude/settings.json` asking for the inner sandbox still wins — a repo asking for more isolation, not less. Found alongside it: the jq branch wrote a **0-byte** `settings.json` on a host with no `~/.claude/settings.json` (jq over `/dev/null` emits nothing), silently dropping every managed key; it now feeds jq `{}`. Guarded by §168, which runs the real seeding block under node, jq-only and no-tool `PATH`s.

#### Suspicious workspaces (`SANDY_SUSPICIOUS`, #130)

The mounted `.credentials.json` carries the **refresh token** — permanent renewable account access until manually revoked — and permissive-mode allowlists (pypi, github) are plausible exfil channels. `SANDY_SUSPICIOUS=1` is the hardened posture for a workspace you distrust:

- **Refresh token stripped** before mounting, so an exfiltrated copy dies at `expiresAt`. The strip is **verified after the rewrite** and **fails closed** — on any error, *no* credentials file is mounted rather than an unstripped one. Trade-off: in-session refresh stops working; relaunch or `/login`.
- **Disposable-key mode**: with `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` set and no long-lived token, the OAuth file is not mounted at all.
- **Connectors forced off** (overriding an approved `=1`) and **egress defaults to strict**; an explicit egress choice still wins but is named loudly.

Passive-safe to turn **on** from a repo (a repo may declare itself suspicious); `=0` from a workspace is approval-gated. **Profile mode is covered too, since 1.12.0 (#252).** `SANDY_CLAUDE_AUTH=profile` clears `CRED_JSON` before the #130 block runs, so every branch there was a no-op for it and the profile's own `credentials/<name>.json` rode in **unmodified, with its `refresh_token`** — a knob documented to shrink blast radius doing nothing for one mode, which is worse than not having it. The ephemeral copy is now stripped in that same block (never the host profile), `cred_mode` becomes **`profile-access-only`**, and a strip that cannot be performed or verified mounts **no profile at all**. **The trap worth knowing if you touch this**: the two file shapes spell the key differently — `.credentials.json` uses camelCase `claudeAiOauth.refreshToken`, a Console profile uses snake_case `refresh_token` (the WIF layout). `_sandy_strip_refresh_token` deleted *and verified* only the camelCase spelling, so reusing it unchanged would have deleted nothing and **returned success** — the same bug wearing a fix's clothes. It now handles both spellings in both halves, and §126(1) is the check that fails if either is dropped.

**Honest limits**: a long-lived `CLAUDE_CODE_OAUTH_TOKEN` is *not* shrunk (recorded honestly and warned about), it is Claude-only, and the access token stays exfiltrable for its remaining TTL. Real prevention is #121 (credential broker). Guarded by §112.

#### Passive config values reach `bash -c` — quote every interpolation (R1, 1.13.1)

`build_claude_cmd` assembles a **string** that is later run by `exec bash -c "$AGENT_CMD 2>&1"`. Two interpolations went in **unquoted**:

```sh
cmd+=" --teammate-mode ${_tm}"      # SANDY_TEAMMATE_MODE
cmd+=" --channels${_ch_specs}"      # SANDY_CHANNELS
```

Both keys are **passive** and neither is in the value-aware gate, so a committed workspace `.sandy/config` sets them with **no approval prompt**. `gh pr checkout N && sandy` was enough, and the payload ran **before the agent's first turn** — ahead of any prompt-injection defence, and invisible to `SANDY_TOOL_AUDIT`, which instruments agent tool calls only. Both chains were demonstrated live on 1.13.0, not inferred: `;`, `&&`, `|` and a bare newline through `--teammate-mode`, and `x;<command>;x` through `--channels`.

**Every other agent's `--model` append already used `printf "%q"`** — these two were simply missed, which is the whole lesson. The rule is therefore the class, not the two lines: **any value interpolated into an agent command string goes through `printf "%q"`**, because the sink is `bash -c`, not an argv array. `SANDY_MODEL` and `SANDY_EFFORT` are safe by a *different* mechanism — both are validated host-side and the launch exits on a bad value — and that asymmetry is exactly why quoting at the sink is the reliable rule rather than remembering which keys are validated.

`SANDY_CHANNELS` is quoted **per token**, not once over the whole string: `_ch_specs` is a space-separated argument list, so a single `%q` over it would collapse the separators into one argument.

Guarded by §128, which asserts the **property** — run the built command and check nothing executed — rather than the presence of a `%q` call, so the next unquoted interpolation fails there. Two details of that section are load-bearing and were both got wrong first: the predicate must distinguish the payload *executing* from its text merely appearing inside an argv echo (a correctly quoted value still contains the marker), and every check must assert the command **ran** — the suite's `set -u` silently killed the probe subshell at `build_claude_cmd`'s unguarded `${SANDY_CHANNELS//,/ }`, so every payload reported "inert" and the section passed vacuously against unquoted code.

#### Agent-layer tool-use audit (`SANDY_TOOL_AUDIT`)

Sandy is otherwise entirely the NDR side — it constrains the box and sees nothing of tool calls. `SANDY_TOOL_AUDIT=1` (passive-safe, default `0`) adds the EDR side: a `PreToolUse` hook running `/usr/local/bin/sandy-tool-audit`, appending one `{ts, tool, args}` JSONL line per call to `~/.claude/tool-audit.jsonl`. The seed uses the **same only-if-absent guard** as `statusLine`, so a user's own hook is never clobbered; the helper **always exits 0** (a non-zero `PreToolUse` would *block* the call) and truncates `tool_input` to 200 chars. **Two limits**: Claude-Code-only, and an audit hook running *inside* the box is not tamper-proof against a determined agent — it is instrumentation for the wrong-but-not-evil adversary. Guarded by §82.

#### Cross-session inbound (`SANDY_CROSS_SESSION_INBOUND`, 1.10.0, experimental)

Maps onto Claude Code's receive-side `crossSessionInbound`, using its own values verbatim: `accept` (delivered with no prompt), `hold` (interactive approval, `[verified pid N]` attribution), `refuse` (sender sees "not accepting").

**Conditional default — load-bearing, and keyed on a DECLARED NEED since 2.4.0 (#380).** Precedence: **explicit > declared need > `refuse`.**

- **Explicit** `SANDY_CROSS_SESSION_INBOUND` always wins, in either direction.
- **Declared need**: unset resolves to `accept` iff a feature manifest *selected for this launch* (D4 — an excluded feature's declaration does not count) carries `"receives": ["cross_session"]` (see "Features" below) — else `refuse`. `receives` is a separate, privileged *statement* of need, independent of whether the feature ships an entry, so a feature can declare it with no entry at all. (Until 2.6.0 it was also documented as "not gated by `SANDY_RELAY`"; that key is now removed.) It is subject to the same non-start cases: headless (`-p`), `--remote` and `--provision` all mean no live session to deliver into, so a declared need still resolves `refuse` under any of them.
- **Otherwise `refuse`.** A workspace with neither a declared need nor an explicit value gets no open receive surface — a feature `entry` alone is **not** enough (see history below). A configured entry that cannot start still fails the launch regardless of `receives`, the same as any other unstartable entry — that rule was never conditioned on this key.

**History: an entry alone used to be enough, on its own.** From 2.4.0 through 2.5.x, a deprecated legacy rule sat between the two bullets above: if no selected feature declared the need, `accept` iff a feature manifest `entry` would actually be started this launch (the pre-2.4.0 rule, from before `receives` existed at all) — so a manifest that shipped only an `entry` and had not yet added `receives` kept delivering unchanged, tagged `cross_session_inbound_source: "relay-legacy"` and warned about at every launch that resolved it. Announced for removal in README's `## Deprecated` table in 2.5.0 under the minor-deprecation exception (#382), it was **removed in 2.6.0** (decision 6) once the one known consumer had migrated to declaring `receives` directly.

The resolved *reason* is recorded in `/etc/sandy-session.json` as `cross_session_inbound_source` (`explicit`|`feature:<name>`|`default`, JSON `null` when `cross_session_inbound` itself is null), so a run's posture is provable after the fact. (`relay-legacy` is a value only a marker written by 2.4.x/2.5.x sandy can carry.)

**Placement — measured, and it is two files.** A live probe against 2.1.251 found `accept` written to *either* workspace settings file is a **no-op**, byte-identical to absence: those files are tighten-only (`hold`/`refuse` DO apply). The only placements that deliver `accept` are Claude Code's **userSettings** and `--settings`. **And since Claude Code 2.1.283 its own *unset* default delivers too** (#379's Q0, `docs/security/CROSS_SESSION_INBOUND.md` §9): with no key in any layer a peer message is routed, so sandy's explicit write is now load-bearing — it is the only thing between a sandy session and that permissive default, and any launch path that skipped it would silently open the receive surface. So sandy writes the same resolved value into both, every launch:

1. `$SANDBOX_DIR/claude/settings.json` (the container's `~/.claude/settings.json`) — the seam that makes `accept` deliver. RW in-container, so a compromised in-session agent could flip its own pin until the next launch (documented residual).
2. `$WORK_DIR/.claude/settings.local.json` — `:ro` in-container, where `hold`/`refuse` take effect and win over the userSettings copy. Writing `accept` here is a delivery no-op but overwrites any stale `hold`/`refuse` sandy wrote earlier, so the two files can never disagree because of sandy's own history.

Both writes are merge-preserving and idempotent, via node → jq → literal-match fallback; a file that is not a JSON object is left untouched with a warning. The workspace write runs before the `:ro` mount loop and the launch snapshot. Sandy **never** writes the host's `~/.claude/settings.json`.

**Neither write follows a symlink (R2, 1.13.2).** The writer read its target with `JSON.parse`/`jq` and `mv -f`'d the result back, and neither step cared that a **repository** had committed the path as a link. Three measured consequences, all reachable by `gh pr checkout N && sandy`, host-side, before `docker run`, with no agent and no approval prompt: `.claude/settings.local.json` → `~/.claude/.credentials.json` made sandy **copy the host refresh token into the workspace** (any JSON object works — `~/.claude.json`, `~/.docker/config.json`, an ADC file); `.claude` committed as a **directory** link made the write land wherever it pointed; and because `mv -f` replaces a link with a regular file, `_sandy_resolve_symlinks` — which on the bare-`sandy` path does not run until ~1800 lines later — **found nothing left to approve**. Sandy's own write consumed the evidence its approval gate keys on.

The rule is now a property of the path, not a containment test: **no component below the anchor (`$WORK_DIR`, or `$SANDBOX_DIR` for the other target) may be a symlink**, walked with bash builtins. A symlink-free chain from the anchor *is* proof the file is inside it, so nothing needs canonicalizing — and `realpath` is GNU-only anyway. Sandy **refuses and warns** rather than resolving or replacing: refusing is what hands the case back to the symlink approval, where an escaping link belongs. Note that "canonicalize, then write" also stops the exfiltration and the escape yet is still wrong for exactly that reason — §129(4) is the check that fails on it. `mkdir -p` cannot be the detector: it succeeds silently on a symlink-to-directory. Guarded by §129.

**Two side effects, stated rather than hidden.** `$WORK_DIR/.claude/settings.local.json` persists on the **host**, so a later plain `claude` in that directory inherits whatever sandy resolved (tighten-only, so not new surface, but a real change outside any sandbox; `rm` it to opt out). And because that write populates `.claude/` before the empty-dir bookkeeping runs, a workspace that had no `.claude/` now keeps one permanently once claude is selected.

**Tier: value-aware.** `hold`/`refuse` are passive-safe and measured to be **real gates** — decided before the sender's in-band attestation is read, so a sender cannot talk past a configured value. `accept` from a workspace is approval-gated.

**Claude-only.** Nothing is written when claude is not in `SANDY_AGENT`. Full threat model, residuals, and the probe record: `docs/security/CROSS_SESSION_INBOUND.md`. Guarded by §114.

**The delivery path is re-measured per Claude Code bump, and the sender frame is a fixture that can go stale.** Re-derived against 2.1.263 (§6a): `accept` logs `[uds-messaging] Routed user message to queue`, `hold` logs `[cross-session-inbound] held … cause=explicit-setting`, `refuse` logs `refused inbound peer message`. Two traps for anyone touching `test/acceptance-uds-delivery.sh`: the **`accept` path emits no `[cross-session-inbound]` line at all**, so absence of one is the success signature and must never be read as non-delivery; and the session key file is JSON — a relay authenticates with its **`peerToken`** field (⇒ class `peer`, which `crossSessionInbound` governs), *not* the file's contents and not `CLAUDE_CODE_MESSAGING_TOKEN`, which is the `childToken` and **bypasses the setting**. Auth is only enforced on Windows, so a wrong token is silently ignored elsewhere rather than failing loudly. **A third trap (#412): the positive case needs the MODEL to act**, which sandy doesn't control. When the sentinel is missing but delivery is proven (the receiver logged `Routed`, logged no hold or refuse, and the case's marker is in the pane), the harness reports a `NOTE`, counted in the RESULT line and the suite summary, instead of a FAIL. Anything short of proven delivery still FAILs. The verdict is the pure function `_uds_positive_outcome`, exercised and mutation-tested by run-tests §189. Both cases poll on one 180 s budget (`UDS_POLL_S`).

#### The relay (a feature manifest `entry`)

**The handoff tree is gone as of 2.2.0.** `#352` removed the `inbox`, `outbox` and `peer` lanes, `#353` moved relay state to `$SANDBOX_DIR/relay-state` (mounted rw at `/opt/sandy/relay-state`), and `#355` removed what was left: the directory, `SANDY_HANDOFF_DIRS`, the `.handoff-enabled` marker, and the `handoff{}`/`handoff_enabled` reporting. A feature manifest names its own directories via `mounts`.

**A supervised process is a manifest `entry`.** The `relay-bin/` slot and `SANDY_HANDOFF_RELAY`-as-a-config-key were removed in `#354`; both are hard errors naming the replacement. **The internal channel `SANDY_HANDOFF_RELAY` later served — carrying an entry's path from host to container-side supervisor — was itself removed in 2.6.0 (#382, decisions 4-5).** The host now passes every adopted entry to the container only via `SANDY_FEATURE_ENTRIES` (internal, no metadata row); the variable `SANDY_HANDOFF_RELAY` is empty at every launch and can only be non-empty when an operator sets it, which is still the hard-error case above.

**`SANDY_RELAY` is a HARD ERROR as of 2.6.0 (#382, decision 3)**, not a capability toggle any more: setting it from any source (environment, host config, workspace config) fails the launch, naming a feature manifest's `"sandboxes": {"exclude": [...]}` as the replacement. There is deliberately no per-feature or workspace-tier off switch. Because there is no way left to suppress an entry, `feature_entries.<name>.disabled_by` had nothing left to record — it is removed, along with `relay{}` itself, as of the same 2.6.0 release (decisions 1-2, below).

**Every selected feature's entry runs, and since 2.6.0 every one is supervised and reported IDENTICALLY (#382, decisions 4-5).** 2.4.0 (#381) first made every entry run (the adoption loop used to take the first `entry` in sorted feature-directory order and silently drop every later one via `elif [ -z "${SANDY_HANDOFF_RELAY:-}" ]` — a second feature's entry was mounted, selected, and never run, with no warning, the #363 failure shape again) but kept a "relay-designated" first entry for backward compatibility with `relay{}` and the internal channel. **2.6.0 removed that designation entirely**: there is no longer a first-among-equals. Every entry gets its own `flock` lock, its own exponential backoff, its own state directory (`$SANDBOX_DIR/feature-state/<feature>`, mounted rw at `/opt/sandy/feature-state/<feature>`, exported to that entry's own process — never the parent shell or any other entry — as `SANDY_FEATURE_STATE`), and its own lock file (`$HOME/.sandy-entry-<feature>.lock`). Exclusivity between consumers is the consumer's own business, enforced where it already is. The launch `features:` line says `entry` for every adopted entry, and a launch adopting more than one adds an info line naming them all — with no `(reported as relay{})` annotation any more, since nothing is designated. §159 guards both (host side: (4) the `features:` line, (5) the `feature entries:` info line); §172 guards the supervisor.

**A leftover `relay-state/` from an older sandy is removed at launch, with one info line.** `$SANDBOX_DIR/relay-state` — the pre-2.6.0 designated entry's shared directory — has no producer any more; a sandbox that still carries one from a pre-2.6.0 launch has it `rm -rf`'d (a symlink is unlinked, never followed) the next time it launches, named once (`Removed leftover relay-state/ …`). Nothing recreates it. `SANDY_RELAY_STATE` is no longer exported, mounted, or read anywhere.

**`relay{}` is REMOVED (2.6.0, #382, decisions 1-2)** — every field, from both the session marker and `--print-state`. It was listed in README's `## Deprecated` table in 2.5.0 (the minor-deprecation exception) while it still dual-reported the first adopted entry — `source`/`path` naming that entry, `disabled_by` recording `SANDY_RELAY=0` when a config source set it — and removed in 2.6.0 once the one known consumer had released its move to `feature_entries`. Its two companion fields, `feature_entries.<name>.relay_alias` and `feature_entries.<name>.disabled_by`, go with it in the **same** bump — an operator decision, since they were never listed separately: `relay_alias` only ever pointed at the entry `relay{}` described, and `disabled_by` only ever recorded `SANDY_RELAY=0`, which decision 3 already made a hard error. Every `feature_entries.<name>` value is now exactly `{path}` in the marker and `{state, restarts, last_exit_code, last_restart_at, executable_present, path, state_dir}` in `--print-state` — every entry reported identically, since there is no longer a designated one.

**Documented exception to "an entry that cannot start fails the launch": a stale image.** Every generated agent Dockerfile carries a `LABEL sandy.feature_entries=1`, inherited through `FROM` into per-project and skill-pack images. An image built before that label existed — a build deferred by `#218`'s reachability gate, or any other old cached image — only ever knew the removed `SANDY_HANDOFF_RELAY` channel, so its `user-setup.sh` starts **none** of the entries (2.6.0, #382, decision 5 — before 2.6.0 it started the relay-designated one, but there is no longer a designated one to fall back to); that is not a broken entry, so it does not fail the launch. With **one or more** entries adopted and the image about to run lacking the label, sandy warns at launch (checked with `docker image inspect -f '{{index .Config.Labels "sandy.feature_entries"}}'`; empty or `<no value>` both count as lacking), naming **every** entry that will not start and pointing at `sandy --rebuild`. Zero entries: silent, since there is nothing a stale image would drop. `--print-state` reports the entries that never started as `state: "absent"`.

**`schema_version` is `4`.** The removals above are all emitted-field removals, and a vanished field is otherwise silent — a consumer gating on a number keeps finding it and merely stops seeing the field. `3` bundled the handoff-tree removals (2.2.0, #355); `4` bundles this release's: `relay{}` in its entirety plus its two companion fields, `relay_alias` and `disabled_by`, described above. Every removal is bundled into one bump because at least one consumer compares `schema_version` by equality, so each bump costs them a refusal and a release.

#### Features: one manifest per feature (2.0.0, `docs/design/FEATURE-MANIFEST.md`)

**Protected submounts (2.8.0, #444).** Optional `submounts` projects a
read-only file or directory beneath a declared feature mount. Entries require
`parent` (a declared mount name), relative `path` (beneath its computed container
destination), and feature-relative `from`; `${slug}` is expanded. `mode` defaults
to `ro` and accepts only `ro`. Optional `agents` contains distinct known agent
names; absent/empty applies to all selected agents. A partial match in a
multi-agent container refuses the whole feature pass. Parents are emitted before
children. Invalid paths, duplicate/overlapping destinations, symlink components,
missing sources or destinations, and file/directory type mismatches refuse
launch. Both endpoints must already exist: no Docker-created destination stub.
The canonical child **source** must neither lie inside nor contain any writable
mount source, including its parent. The canonical child **destination** must
neither lie inside nor contain any other writable mount source; its own parent
is the necessary exception for that destination check only. Thus `work/data` →
`work/results` is refused when `work` is writable. Keep protected sources in a
separate tree, outside writable mount sources. `--print-schema` publishes
`manifest.submount_keys` and `submounts` in `manifest.top_level_keys`; consumers
gate on membership, with `schema_version` still `4`. Guarded by §202, including
the full launch's mount-record-to-`-v` wiring and scratch-copy mutation checks.


`$SANDY_HOME/features/<name>/feature.json` declares everything about a feature: which sandboxes it applies to, what to create per sandbox, what to mount, what to export, and what to supervise. **Privileged by construction of where it lives** — a repository cannot reach `$SANDY_HOME` — so there is no new tier and no approval prompt.

**Selection is enrolment, evaluated at every launch.** An include must match in **both** the `sandboxes` and `agents` blocks and no exclude may match in **either**; exclude wins. Default-deny: a block with no include selects nothing. A sandbox that is not selected gets **no mount, no export, no entry** — that is the security property, and §136(3) is the check. `agents` is applied *at* the launch rather than read from last-launch data, which is the caveat it retires. Multi-agent: mounts are per-container, not per-pane, so an include matches if **any** resolved agent matches and an exclude fires if **any** does — exclude is the strict side, because an excluded agent would otherwise share the container with the payload.

**Sandy never parses JSON itself.** `node` or `jq` projects the manifest to flat TAB records and bash reads those. **Strict parse or refuse**: a launch that must read a manifest and finds neither refuses, and a manifest that cannot be read *in full* mounts nothing. This deliberately does **not** reuse `json_merge`, whose `command -v node &>/dev/null || return 0` silently does nothing — that shape must never reach a mount decision. The two projectors are held byte-identical by §135(20), because the jq one silently dropped the unknown-key check on its first draft: on a jq-only host a typo'd `mounts` would have been ignored rather than refused.

**`agent_args` (2.1.0, #348) lets a manifest contribute launch flags**, keyed by agent, passed at the launch that selects the sandbox — stateless (nothing written into the sandbox, nothing to reap) and read-only by construction (every file named lives on the `:ro` payload). Named `agent_args` rather than `args` because the manifest already has `entry` and `args` beside it reads as *the entry's* arguments. Feature args are **additive, ordered first, never suppressible**; several features compose in sorted slug order; only `-p`/`--print`/`--prompt` are filtered, with the warning naming the feature. Sandy defines the ORDER — precedence belongs to the agent's parser **where that parser has a precedence to apply**, which is not every flag (see below). A token containing whitespace is **refused, never split**, and like any refusal it takes the whole manifest down, so `--print-schema` now publishes `manifest.top_level_keys` for a consumer to gate on by MEMBERSHIP (a stripped `X.Y.Z-dev` compares equal to `X.Y.Z`, so a version gate is worthless here). What was applied is recorded per feature in the session marker and surfaced by `--print-state`, with three non-collapsible states: absent = a sandy that cannot answer, `{}` = none applied, populated = what and by whom. Guarded by §148.

**`agent_args` composes in the argv sandy BUILDS; whether it composes in EFFECT is the agent's parser's business, and for some flags it does not (2.3.0, #363).** A flag the parser reads **once** silently discards every occurrence but the last. That shipped: two features each contributed `--append-system-prompt-file`, sandy passed both in feature order, claude kept the second, and the first feature's prompt never reached the system prompt. Nothing caught it, because **every observable read healthy** — all mounts present and `:ro`, and the marker recorded *both* features, since the marker records what was **passed**. 2.1.0's own rationale is where the hole was: it justified "precedence belongs to the parser" with `--mcp-config` being variadic, generalizing one repeatable flag into an assumption about all of them.

Measured on Claude Code 2.1.278 (`FLAG exists FLAG /nope` errors on `/nope`; the reverse order does not): `--append-system-prompt-file`, `--append-subagent-system-prompt-file` and `--system-prompt-file` are **last-wins**; `--mcp-config`, `--add-dir`, `--plugin-dir` and `--plugin-dir-no-mcp` genuinely accumulate (explicit collectors in the bundle). **Re-measure on every Claude Code bump** — same discipline `acceptance-uds-delivery.sh` carries for the delivery path.

So sandy keeps a small published policy table (`--print-schema` → `manifest.agent_args_compose`), and **only for a flag listed there**:

- **`concat`** — the flag names a text file, so at **two or more** contributors sandy merges them in the order it would have passed them into `$SANDBOX_DIR/launch/<id>/agent-args/<agent>.<flag>.md` (a fresh path per launch, #400), mounts that `:ro` at `/opt/sandy/agent-args/`, and passes the flag **once**. Each section is headed with the contributing feature.
- **`report`** — the flag *replaces* rather than appends, so there is no correct merge: sandy names the collision and the loser and changes nothing.
- **not listed** — sandy assumes the agent accumulates and does nothing, which is every other flag of every agent.

**Nothing happens at one contributor**, and that is the whole safety argument: the argv is byte-identical to 2.2.0 and the feature's own live mount is what the agent reads. A value sandy cannot resolve to a host file through its own mount table (an operator `agent-args.<agent>` may name anything) is **reported but not merged** — fail open on what sandy cannot see, while still proving the collision. The composed file lives under `/opt/sandy`, not inside any feature root, for #353's reason: it is sandy's derivation from several features and belongs to none of them.

**`agent_args_composed` in the marker and `--print-state` is the half that makes this visible**, and it is a different question from `agent_args`: that one records what was **passed**, this one whether it can have taken **effect**. Entries carry `composed: true|false` so "merged" and "collision reported, argv unchanged" never collapse. Additive, so `schema_version` does not move. Guarded by §153, every check mutation-tested.

**And the launch now SAYS which features applied**, without `SANDY_VERBOSE`. A selected feature used to print nothing and a skip was verbose-only, so "installed" and "applied to this sandbox" were indistinguishable at launch — which is half of why the dropped prompt above took a debugging round to even confirm as applied. One line names each applied feature and what it contributed (mount count, which agents got args, whether it supplied an `entry`); a second names anything not applied **and why**. A `skip` for a feature that *was* selected means a declared mount whose source is missing, which is a real misconfiguration and warns; the `summary` record per selected feature is what tells those two apart.

**`receives` (2.4.0, #380) lets a manifest declare a NEED, never a consumer or a mechanism.** An array from a closed, published set — today exactly `["cross_session"]`, `--print-schema` → `manifest.receives_values` — not a string and not a boolean: a second kind of inbound need is a new enum value, not a schema change, and an unknown value **refuses the whole manifest**, naming it, the same as any other key. `[]` is valid and declares nothing. `"receives": ["cross_session"]` is what keys `SANDY_CROSS_SESSION_INBOUND`'s unset default (see "Cross-session inbound" above) — for a **selected** feature only (D4; an excluded feature's declaration does not count), and independent of any entry — `receives` is a separate, privileged *statement* of need, so a feature can declare it with no `entry` at all. Both projectors and the shell copy (`_sandy_fm_receives_known`) are held byte-identical the same way the rest of the manifest's key lists are (§173, one clause after §135(20)).
**The manifest declares a `name`, never a destination.** Sandy computes it — `payload` → `/opt/sandy/features/<feature>`, `.` → `<home>/.<feature>`, anything else → `<home>/.<feature>/<name>`. There is no `to` field, so there is nothing to police; an earlier draft had one and would have permitted a mount over `/etc/sandy-session.json`. `name` and every `from` segment go through `[A-Za-z0-9._-]+`, no leading dot, no `..` — a computed root is only a root if its components cannot escape it. `mode` defaults to `ro`. One reserved top-level key, `feature`, is never interpreted; every other unknown key is **refused**, because a typo'd `mounts` that mounts nothing silently is the failure class this project spent three releases removing.

**`selected.json`** beside each manifest records which sandboxes were selected and why the others were not, for a consumer that executes nothing and so cannot run `--print-state`. It is per-slug files rendered with `printf`, not a read-modify-write, so `--remove-sandbox` can drop an entry with no parser on the host. **The note ships inside the file**: selection depends on the agents a launch resolved to, so for a sandbox that is not running the agent dimension is **unknowable**, not merely stale.

**Retired in 2.0.0**: the `features/<name>` per-sandbox marker and `SANDY_FEATURES_DIR` (now a hard error naming the replacement). `--print-state`'s `features` keeps its name with a **different source**, which is why `schema_version` moved to `2`. `--remove-sandbox` reaps the feature-owned instance tree and selection record; `--reset-sandbox` leaves them, because a reset keeps the sandbox.

**History (2.2.0–2.5.x; retired in 2.6.0 — see "The relay (a feature manifest `entry`)" above for the current shape). Relay state moved out of `handoff/` in 2.2.0 (#353).** `.state` and `supervisor.log` lived at `$SANDBOX_DIR/relay-state`, mounted **rw** at `/opt/sandy/relay-state`. Producer-agnostic by design: an earlier draft proposed the feature's own instance tree, which `relay.source` disproved (two of the three producers had no feature) — and the reason that outlives even that is simpler, and the consumer's own: **these were sandy's supervisor's files, not the relay's**, so putting them in a feature's tree would have made sandy's bookkeeping a tenant of the thing it supervises. Under `/opt/sandy`, not the agent home, because `~/.sandy` in-container would read as the **host** config root.

**History, same window.** The container path had exactly one source: the host set `SANDY_RELAY_STATE` beside the mount and the supervisor read it. A relay located its state by reading that variable, never by constructing a path — *"consumer constructs a path sandy owns"* had by then been the fault line twice already (#248 moved the home, #345 derived `relay.path` from the slot) and #353 moved this too. `--print-state` reported the directory as `relay.state_dir`, a **host** path — `relay.path` in the same object was a **container** path, and the two frames were not to be conflated. A one-time `mv` migrated an existing sandbox and ran **before** the `handoff/relay` mkdir; the other order would have moved the empty directory `mkdir` just created, on every launch. **2.6.0 (#382) removed all of this**: `SANDY_RELAY_STATE`, `relay-state/`, and `relay.state_dir`/`relay.path` in `--print-state` are gone; each entry now reports its own `state_dir` under `feature_entries.<name>`, sourced from `SANDY_FEATURE_STATE`.

**The coexistence window CLOSED in 2.2.0 (#354).** The `relay-bin/` slot and `SANDY_HANDOFF_RELAY`-as-a-configuration-key are both removed; a manifest `entry` is the only producer. A leftover slot entry, or an operator setting `SANDY_HANDOFF_RELAY`, is a **hard error naming the replacement** — never a silent skip, because ignoring either would start the wrong relay, or none, without saying so. `--reset-sandbox` now **destroys** `relay-bin/` rather than preserving it, since a surviving entry blocks the next launch.

**The variable is not the key.** `SANDY_HANDOFF_RELAY` remained the internal channel a manifest `entry` travelled through to the container-side supervisor through 2.5.x, so a change that deleted the variable would have deleted the entry mechanism with it. The hard error fires only for a value present *before* the manifest block runs — which can only have come from an operator. **This whole paragraph, and the two mount/state paragraphs above it, describe the 2.2.0-2.5.x mechanism and are kept for the reasoning; 2.6.0 (#382, decisions 4-5) retired `relay-state/`, `SANDY_RELAY_STATE` and the internal channel itself — see "The relay (a feature manifest `entry`)" above for the current shape, where every entry uses `$SANDBOX_DIR/feature-state/<feature>` and `SANDY_FEATURE_STATE` identically, and a leftover `relay-state/` is removed on sight.**

**`SANDY_RELAY` was RE-SCOPED, not retired — this is the 2.1.0-through-2.5.x history; see "`SANDY_RELAY` is a HARD ERROR" above for the current, 2.6.0 shape.** It was never listed in README's `## Deprecated` table and entries may only be **added** there in an `X.0.0`, so removing it in a minor was exactly what §138 existed to prevent — until 2.5.0's written exception (above) covered it, and 2.6.0 (decision 3) made it a hard error outright. Its documented meaning was never slot-specific — *"run the installed relay if there is one"* — so with the slot gone it gated the only remaining producer. That was a behaviour change in the **tightening** direction: `=0` used to disable a slot relay and silently **not** stop a manifest entry, then it stopped both, with the suppression **loud** (named at launch, `disabled_by` recorded — that field is itself gone as of 2.6.0, decisions 1-2), because a fleet going silently dark is the failure the window existed to prevent.

*The paragraph below describes the window that has now closed, kept for the reasoning.*

**Coexistence window**: `entry` and `relay-bin/relay` both work for one release and **`relay-bin` wins**, with a notice — a flag day on the mechanism that starts a delivery daemon is how a fleet goes silently dark. The `handoff/` tree, `SANDY_HANDOFF_*` and the `handoff_relay`/`relay{}` session keys also survive 2.0, deprecated.

### Forwarding env vars and agent args

#### `SANDY_EXTRA_ENV`

Comma-separated env-var **names** to forward for user-installed MCP servers or in-container tooling sandy has no opinion on:

```sh
SANDY_EXTRA_ENV=HA_TOKEN,LINEAR_API_KEY   # ~/.sandy/config (privileged)
HA_TOKEN=ey...                            # ~/.sandy/.secrets, or the shell env
```

**The name lists compose (2.4.0, #388)**: the effective list is the union of the host lists, any **approved** workspace list, and an env-set list — deduplicated, host names first, then names the workspace adds, then names env adds. An env-set `SANDY_EXTRA_ENV` **adds** rather than replaces; it is the one key where env is not a complete override, because replacement is the silent drop being fixed — under last-wins a workspace forwarding one token of its own silently dropped every host-forwarded name. The config loader records the per-source lists instead of exporting them, and the approval step only marks the workspace lists admitted, so neither can re-introduce last-wins. The launch names the sources when more than one contributes. Guarded by §165.

Value resolution (per name, unchanged): `env > workspace/.sandy/.secrets > workspace/.sandy/config > ~/.sandy/.secrets > ~/.sandy/config`. Env wins absolutely; among files workspace beats host, and within a tier `.secrets` beats `config`.

**The security boundary is on the names, not the values** — `SANDY_EXTRA_ENV` is privileged, so a workspace setting it triggers the approval prompt; once `HA_TOKEN` is approved the value may come from anywhere. Names must match `[A-Z_][A-Z0-9_]*`; names matching a sandy-recognized key are skipped (they have their own typed path); a listed name with no value warns but does not fail the launch.

#### `SANDY_AGENT_ARGS`

Fixed extra CLI flags for the underlying agent on **every** launch — including `sandy -p`, `--start`, and programmatic callers like sandy-ui, which a shell wrapper cannot cover.

```sh
SANDY_AGENT_ARGS=--mcp-config .mcp.custom.json --some-experimental-flag value
```

- Resolved into per-agent env channels the container-side dispatcher prepends to each pane's command, so **by default every selected agent gets the same value**. Final order: sandy's own flags → `SANDY_AGENT_ARGS` (or the per-agent file) → command-line pass-through args, so an explicit CLI arg still wins.
- **Agent-agnostic is a footgun for agent-specific flags**: a Claude-Code-only flag here makes a `SANDY_AGENT=codex` launch print codex's usage banner and exit, which reads like a sandy fault and is not one. Not hypothetical — sandy's own `.sandy/config` failed the integration suite's codex section exactly this way. Put agent-specific flags in the per-agent file below.
- **Parsing (v1)**: whitespace-split into argv (`read -ra`), never `eval`'d, then `printf %q`-quoted downstream. **Embedded spaces / quoted args are not supported.**
- **Privileged tier** — arbitrary agent flags from a committed config are a real attack surface (`--dangerously-skip-permissions`, a hostile `--mcp-config`, `--add-dir` escapes). Headless/non-TTY drops it. **Headless-mode flags (`-p`/`--print`/`--prompt`) are dropped with a warning** — host-side headless detection runs before this injection, so a `-p` here would make the host launch interactive while the container went headless. Avoid `--continue`/`--new` here too.

#### Per-agent override: `$SANDBOX_DIR/agent-args.<agent>`

The same capability from a place a git repository cannot reach (`agent-args.claude`, `.codex`, `.gemini`, `.opencode`, `.grok`, `.mirror`).

- **Top level, deliberately** — `$SANDBOX_DIR/claude` is mounted rw at `~/.claude`, so a file there would be **agent-writable**, and an agent that can grant itself a flag can grant itself anything that flag allows. Asserted structurally by §106.
- **No new tier, no prompt**: `$SANDY_HOME` is already the privileged config root, so the file is privileged by construction of *where it lives* — like `.handoff-enabled`.
- **The sandbox file wins**, per agent, over `SANDY_AGENT_ARGS`. Sandy discards source attribution at the approval step, so it cannot tell where a surviving value came from; rather than guess or merge, it refuses the ambiguity — the file's tokens are used verbatim and a notice names the winning source. Values are **never concatenated**.
- **Empty or whitespace-only = absent** (the predicate is "any surviving token after filtering", never `[ -s ]`). Newlines are whitespace, so one argument per line works and every line survives. Mode flags are dropped with a warning naming the file.
- Per-pane isolation is the point: `agent-args.codex` never reaches the claude pane. `sandy --remote` is covered too. Files for an unselected agent are silently ignored (only `--print-state` surfaces them, via `agent_args_files` — presence only, not effective args). `--reset-sandbox` preserves them; `--remove-sandbox` names each in the plan and destroys them.

### Skill Packs

Optional Docker layers baking curated skill collections into the container. Not included by default.

```sh
SANDY_SKILL_PACKS=gstack
```

| Pack | Description | Source |
|------|-------------|--------|
| `gstack` | 28 Claude Code skills (QA, review, ship, browse, …) + headless Chromium | [garrytan/gstack](https://github.com/garrytan/gstack) |

Two build phases sit between the agent image and the per-project image: **2.5a** (`sandy-skills-base-{pack}`) installs heavy, rarely-changing deps (Playwright, Chromium) and rebuilds when its Dockerfile changes **or its parent image moves**; **2.5b** (`sandy-skills-{pack}`) fetches the pinned source and builds, rebuilding on a new version but fast because Chromium is already cached. At startup `user-setup.sh` symlinks `/opt/skills/{pack}/` into `~/.claude/skills/` and adds pack `bin/` dirs to PATH.

**Version resolution** happens per launch, with no hardcoded pin: GitHub releases API (latest non-draft/non-prerelease matching the pack's tag prefix, 5s timeout) → GitHub commits API → local cache `~/.sandy/.skill_version_{pack}` → the `SKILL_PACK_VERSIONS` fallback array. A new version regenerates `Dockerfile.skills`, changing its hash and triggering only the 2.5b rebuild.

**Each skills cache key folds in its parent image id (2.1.0, #294).** Both images are built `FROM` an image sandy also builds, but each used to rebuild only when its *own* Dockerfile changed — so once `sandy-claude-code` moved, a skill-pack sandbox kept launching with whatever `user-setup.sh` and `entrypoint.sh` were baked in at its last build. Measured in the field: a gstack base from 2026-07-20 carried a `user-setup.sh` predating the relay supervisor (1.10.0), so every launch mounted the relay slot, exported `SANDY_HANDOFF_RELAY`, started the agent — **and never started the supervisor**, because the script that would start it was not in the image. `--print-state` then reported `relay.state="failed"` for a sandbox that had done nothing wrong. `BUILD_HASH` for the agent images already hashes those two files for exactly this reason; the skills chain inherits them through `FROM` and so never saw them change. `_sandy_parent_image_id` reads the `FROM` **out of the generated Dockerfile** rather than reconstructing the name — `Dockerfile.skills` is `FROM` the base image when a pack needs one and `FROM sandy-claude-code` when none does. A parent docker cannot resolve hashes as `absent`, never as an empty string: empty would hash identically to a missing parent and silently stop invalidating. Guarded by §150.

**gstack state** lives at `<workspace>/.gstack/` on the host (workspace-scoped, beside `.git/`), auto-created at launch, with a one-line warning if it is not gitignored. Pre-0.12 sandy used `$SANDBOX_DIR/gstack/`; the first launch after upgrading `cp -a`s it over and renames the legacy dir to `gstack.migrated/` (manual cleanup once confirmed).

**Adding a pack**: entries in `SKILL_PACK_NAMES`, `SKILL_PACK_REPOS`, `SKILL_PACK_VERSIONS`, `SKILL_PACK_TAG_PREFIXES`, plus a build recipe case in `generate_skill_pack_dockerfiles()`.

### Workspace mounts and misc

**Workspace mount path**: mounted at a path mirroring the host's `$HOME`-relative location — `~/dev/sandy` → `/home/sandy/dev/sandy`. Outside `$HOME` it falls back to the real host path. Passed as `SANDY_WORKSPACE`.

**Git submodules**: sandy detects the `.git` file (vs directory), resolves the relative gitdir, and mounts both worktree and gitdir at the correct container paths using the same mapping to preserve the relative relationship.

**The `gitdir:` line is repository content, and it is validated (R5, 1.13.3).** It used to be resolved with a bare `cd` and no containment check, then bind-mounted with **no `:ro`**. Measured: `gitdir: ../../.ssh` produced `-v $HOME/.ssh:/home/sandy/.ssh` **read-write**, and `gitdir: /etc` produced `-v /etc:/etc` **read-write**. Write access to `~/.ssh` is host code execution — an `~/.ssh/config` `ProxyCommand` fires on the user's next `git push`. The symlink approval cannot see any of it: `.git` is a regular **file**, not a link, so the scan finds nothing.

The gate is **structural, not a path prefix**, and that choice is load-bearing: a legitimate submodule gitdir is deliberately *outside* `$WORK_DIR` (it lives under the superproject) and a `--separate-git-dir` may be anywhere the user chose, so any "must be under X" rule rejects real setups. *Is it actually a git directory* — `HEAD` plus `objects/` plus `refs/` — is the property that separates those from `~/.ssh`, `~/.aws` and `/etc`, and it needs no canonicalization. A directory an attacker could already furnish with that layout is one they can already write, so the check gives nothing away. A failure is a hard error with the `.fatal` marker, so `--start` fails in ~1s rather than polling out its timeout.

**The gitdir is mounted rw, so its own `config`, `hooks/` and `info/` are now overlaid `:ro`** — git must write the gitdir (index, refs, objects), but the host-code-execution surfaces inside it need the same protection every other repo gets. Nothing covered them before: `_protect_submodule_gitdirs` walks `$GITDIR_HOST/**modules**` (gitdirs *nested* under this one) and never `$GITDIR_HOST` itself, and `_sandy_protected_git_files` targets `$WORK_DIR/.git/config`, which does not exist when `.git` is a file. Measured on a real submodule worktree: the gitdir produced exactly **one** mount flag, read-write, with no `:ro` over `config` or `hooks/`. So a wholly trustworthy repo still handed the agent host-write access to that submodule's hooks. Guarded by §130.

**Screenshots / `/ss`**: `SANDY_SCREENSHOT_DIR=<host-path>` (privileged) mounts a host screenshot folder `:ro` at `/home/sandy/screenshots`, exposed as `$SANDY_SCREENSHOTS_PATH`, and generates a per-agent `/ss` surface at sandbox setup (claude: `~/.claude/commands/ss.md`; gemini: `~/.gemini/commands/ss.toml`; codex: a description-matched skill; opencode: manual). All are powered by `/usr/local/bin/sandy-ss-paths`, callable from any agent's bash escape. Validation rejects shell metacharacters and overly-broad targets (literal `$HOME`, `/`); a non-existent directory is warn-and-skip, so Docker never auto-creates an empty stub on the host. No default — unset disables the feature entirely.

**Status lines** — two, at different scopes because neither can do the other's job. The **outer tmux bar** is launch/session-scoped, rendering live from env via `#{E:VAR}` (egress posture color-coded, agents, project, attached-client count, daemon/session marker, clock); window-status is blanked since sandy sessions are multi-*pane*. The **inner Claude Code `statusLine`** is live and per-request — model, effort, context% — which the outer bar structurally cannot show, since that is Claude Code's own state fed on stdin per render. Sandy seeds it to `/usr/local/bin/sandy-claude-statusline` in all three seeding branches with an **only-if-absent guard**, and the helper falls back to a bare `sandy` line on any malformed input rather than erroring the TUI.

**Container timezone (2.4.0, #384)**: the host zone is passed as a runtime `-e TZ=` (resolved from `$TZ` → `readlink /etc/localtime` → `/etc/timezone`; never a build input, never a config key — the host's `$TZ` *is* the override), and the entrypoint unsets a zone the image lacks. **Everything sandy emits as data stays UTC** — any new timestamp producer must use `date -u` (or epoch seconds); §163(17) runs every `$(date …)` in the script under `TZ=Pacific/Kiritimati` and fails on a local one, and §163(18) is a static ratchet over every `date` and mtime-`stat` call, heredocs included, for the forms (17) cannot execute (a `|| date` fallback, a backtick `date`, `stat -c %y` without `TZ=UTC`).

**Terminal notifications**: inner tmux sets `allow-passthrough on`, forwarding OSC 9/99/777 from Claude Code to the outer terminal (cmux, iTerm2). Host-side `~/.claude/hooks/` is mounted `:ro` into the container so host-configured hooks work without duplication.

**Pane resize keys** (#161, 2.4.0): `tmux.conf` binds `prefix` + `H`/`J`/`K`/`L` (`bind -r`, 5 cells) — the only bindings sandy adds — because tmux's stock resize keys fail on macOS out of the box (`prefix` + Ctrl-Arrow is Mission Control's; `prefix` + M-Arrow needs Option-as-Meta). `L` shadows stock `switch-client -l`, harmless in sandy's single session. `tmux.conf` is in the agent image hash, so editing it costs users one rebuild. Guarded by §161, which loads the file into a private-socket tmux server and reads back `list-keys`.
